    `BEGIN condition WHILE ... REPEAT` (repeats while condition is true).

31.6 Variables, Constants, and Memory Access
--------------------------------------------
  - Constants:
      `100 CONSTANT MAX-LIVES`
      Typing `MAX-LIVES` immediately places 100 onto the stack.
  - Variables:
      `VARIABLE SCORE`
      Typing `SCORE` places the *memory address* of the variable on the stack.
  - Storing and Fetching:
      `!` (Store 16-bit):  `500 SCORE !`   (Stores 500 into SCORE)
      `@` (Fetch 16-bit):  `SCORE @ .`     (Fetches and prints 500)
      `+!` (Add to RAM) :  `50 SCORE +!`   (Adds 50 to SCORE)
  - Single-Byte Access:
      `C@` (Fetch byte from address) and `C!` (Store byte to address).

31.7 Sinclair Spectrum Hardware Words in BasinPy Forth
------------------------------------------------------
BasinPy includes dedicated words to control the Sinclair ZX Spectrum hardware directly:
  - Graphics:
      `CLS`                    Clear graphics and text canvas
      `x y PLOT`               Plot pixel at coordinate (x, y)
      `dx dy DRAW`             Draw line relative to current position
      `x y radius CIRCLE`      Draw circle
  - Colors:
      `color BORDER`           Set border color (0..7)
      `color INK`              Set text/drawing ink color
      `color PAPER`            Set paper color
  - Sound:
      `pitch duration BEEP`    Generate authentic Sinclair beeper tone
      `reg val SOUND`          Write directly to AY-3-8912 sound chip registers
  - Direct Memory & I/O:
      `addr PEEK`              Read byte from memory
      `val addr POKE`          Write byte to memory
      `port IN`                Read hardware I/O port
      `val port OUT`           Write to hardware I/O port

31.8 Native Z80 Compilation & Exporting (.ZXF Containers)
---------------------------------------------------------
BasinPy compiles Forth definitions into ultra-fast native Z80 machine code cached
in high RAM. You can save your entire Forth environment and programs into native
Sinclair Forth `.ZXF` containers:
  - Saving: `SAVE "starfield", FORTH`
  - Loading: `LOAD "starfield", FORTH`
  - Listing resident Forth blocks: `LIST CODE` in the IDE listing pane displays the
    starting address and byte length of all compiled Forth machine code blocks!

31.9 Hands-On Example: The Bouncing Star
----------------------------------------
Enter `FORTH` mode and type this complete program:
```forth
VARIABLE STAR-X
VARIABLE STAR-DX

: INIT-STAR
  10 STAR-X !
  1 STAR-DX !
  CLS 1 BORDER 7 INK ;

: MOVE-STAR
  STAR-X @ 20 > IF -1 STAR-DX ! THEN
  STAR-X @ 2 <  IF  1 STAR-DX ! THEN
  STAR-DX @ STAR-X +! ;

: DRAW-STAR
  0 0 AT
  STAR-X @ SPACES ." *" CR ;

: RUN-STAR
  INIT-STAR
  50 0 DO
    DRAW-STAR
    MOVE-STAR
    50 1 BEEP
  LOOP ;
```
Type `RUN-STAR` to watch the star bounce smoothly across the screen accompanied by
real-time Spectrum beeps! Type `BYE` to return to Sinclair BASIC.


================================================================================
 33. SECTION 32: SINCLAIR MICRO-PROLOG BEGINNER'S GUIDE & LANGUAGE REFERENCE
================================================================================

PROLOG (Programming in Logic) is the premier artificial intelligence and symbolic
reasoning language. Created in the 1970s by Alain Colmerauer and Robert Kowalski,
Prolog was chosen in the 1980s by the Japanese Fifth Generation Computer Systems
initiative as the foundation for future intelligent machines.

Logic Programming represents a profound paradigm shift:
  - In Procedural Languages (BASIC, C): You tell the computer *how* to solve a problem step-by-step.
  - In Prolog: You declare *facts* and *rules* about a world, and the computer uses
    logical deduction (unification and backtracking) to find the answers for you!

BasinPy implements a full Sinclair Micro-PROLOG system supporting both the classic
1980s Sinclair Micro-PROLOG list syntax and standard Edinburgh / ISO syntax.

32.1 Running Micro-PROLOG in BasinPy
-----------------------------------
  - Interactive Prolog Shell:
      Type `PROLOG` at the BASIC prompt. The prompt changes to `?- ` (or `&.`),
      indicating that the logic inference engine is ready.
      Type `BYE`, `QUIT`, or `BASIC` to return to Sinclair BASIC.
  - Embedded Prolog in Sinclair BASIC Programs:
      ```basic
      10 CLS
      20 PROLOG
      30   human(socrates).
      40   mortal(X) :- human(X).
      50   ?- mortal(socrates).
      60 END PROLOG
      70 PRINT "Prolog reasoning complete!"
      ```

32.2 Syntax and Fundamentals: Atoms, Variables, and Facts
---------------------------------------------------------
A Prolog knowledge base consists of clauses (facts and rules):

  - Atoms (Constants):
    Named entities, lower-case words or symbols:
      `socrates`, `zeus`, `sinclair`, `london`, `computer`, `red`
  - Variables:
    Placeholders that the engine can bind to any matching value. Variables start with
    an uppercase letter or an underscore, or single letters `x`, `y`, `z`, `who`, `what`:
      `X`, `Person`, `Item`, `_temp`, `who`, `what`
  - Dual Syntax Compatibility:
    BasinPy recognizes both standard Edinburgh syntax and classic Micro-PROLOG syntax:
    * Edinburgh Syntax:     `parent(john, mary).`
    * Micro-PROLOG Syntax:  `((parent john mary))`

32.3 Building a Knowledge Base: ADD, DELETE, LIST, NEW
------------------------------------------------------
When inside interactive `PROLOG` mode:
  - Adding Facts:
      `ADD human(socrates).`
      `ADD human(plato).`
      `ADD human(aristotle).`
      `ADD greek(socrates).`
  - Adding Rules:
      `ADD mortal(X) :- human(X).`
      (Read as: "X is mortal IF X is human")
  - Listing the Database:
      `LIST ALL`              Display all stored facts and rules
      `LIST human`            Display clauses for relation `human`
  - Deleting Clauses:
      `DELETE human 2`        Delete the 2nd clause of relation `human`
      `KILL human`            Delete the entire `human` relation
  - Clearing Memory:
      `NEW`                   Clear entire database for a fresh start
  - Saving and Loading:
      `SAVE "family"`         Save clauses to `family.pro`
      `LOAD "family"`         Load clauses from disk

32.4 Querying the Database: `?`, `WHICH`, `ALL`, `ONE`
------------------------------------------------------
Once you have defined your facts and rules, you can pose questions to the engine:

  - Truth Queries (`?` or `?-`):
      `?- human(socrates).`
      -> `YES`
      `?- human(zeus).`
      -> `NO`

  - Variable Queries (`WHICH`, `ALL`):
      `?- WHICH (X human(X))`
      -> `socrates`
      -> `plato`
      -> `aristotle`
      -> `No more solutions.`

      `?- ALL (X human(X))`
      -> `[socrates, plato, aristotle]`

32.5 Defining Complex Rules & Relationships (Family Tree Case Study)
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Rules allow Prolog to deduce new knowledge from existing facts. Consider this
classic family relationship database:
```prolog
parent(pam, bob).
parent(tom, bob).
parent(tom, liz).
parent(bob, ann).
parent(bob, pat).
parent(pat, jim).

female(pam).
female(liz).
female(ann).
female(pat).
male(tom).
male(bob).
male(jim).

mother(M, C) :- parent(M, C), female(M).
father(F, C) :- parent(F, C), male(F).

grandparent(GP, GC) :- parent(GP, P), parent(P, GC).
grandfather(GF, GC) :- grandparent(GF, GC), male(GF).

sibling(X, Y) :- parent(P, X), parent(P, Y), X \= Y.
```
Now pose queries:
  - `?- grandfather(tom, jim).` -> `YES`
  - `?- WHICH (X sibling(bob, X))` -> `liz`

32.6 Working with Lists and Pattern Matching
--------------------------------------------
In Prolog, lists are ordered sequences written in square brackets `[item1, item2, ...]`.
The bar symbol `|` separates the first element (`Head`) from the remainder of the list (`Tail`):
  - `[H | T]` matches `[apple, banana, cherry]` with `H = apple` and `T = [banana, cherry]`.

  - Testing List Membership (`member`):
      `member(X, [X | _]).`
      `member(X, [_ | Tail]) :- member(X, Tail).`
      Query: `?- member(2, [1, 2, 3]).` -> `YES`
      Query: `?- WHICH (X member(X, [red, green, blue]))` -> `red`, `green`, `blue`

  - Concatenating Lists (`append`):
      `append([], L, L).`
      `append([H | T], L, [H | R]) :- append(T, L, R).`
      Query: `?- append([1, 2], [3, 4], X).` -> `X = [1, 2, 3, 4]`

32.7 Built-in Predicates Reference
----------------------------------
  - Arithmetic Evaluation (`is`):
      `X is 10 + 25` -> `X = 35`
      Supports `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, `mod`.
  - Comparisons:
      `X = Y` (unification), `X \= Y` (not equal), `X < Y`, `X > Y`, `X =< Y`, `X >= Y`.
  - Cut (`!`):
      Prunes backtracking search choices to commit to a specific solution branch.
  - Negation as Failure (`NOT` or `\+`):
      `orphan(X) :- person(X), \+ parent(_, X).`
  - Input & Output:
      `P("Text")`             Print text
      `PP(Term)`              Pretty-print term with newlines
      `R(X)`                  Read user keyboard input into variable `X`

32.8 Hands-On Project: Expert Hardware Diagnostic System
--------------------------------------------------------
Enter `PROLOG` and type this practical troubleshooting advisor:
```prolog
symptom(no_power) :- P("Is power LED lit? (yes/no): "), R(no).
symptom(black_screen) :- P("Is screen pitch black with border? (yes/no): "), R(yes).
symptom(flashing_garbage) :- P("Are flashing blocks on screen? (yes/no): "), R(yes).

fault(faulty_power_supply) :- symptom(no_power).
fault(faulty_ula_chip) :- symptom(black_screen).
fault(lower_ram_failure) :- symptom(flashing_garbage).

diagnose :-
  fault(Diagnosis),
  P("DIAGNOSIS: "), PP(Diagnosis), !.
diagnose :-
  P("DIAGNOSIS: Unknown hardware issue. Check bus connections.").
```
Type `?- diagnose.` and answer the questions interactively to watch Prolog diagnose
ZX Spectrum hardware issues! Type `BYE` to return to BASIC.


================================================================================
 34. SECTION 33: SINCLAIR LOGO TURTLE GRAPHICS USER GUIDE
================================================================================

Sinclair LOGO brings the brilliant educational philosophy of Seymour Papert and
MIT's Artificial Intelligence Laboratory to BasinPy. Rather than struggling with
abstract Cartesian coordinates, LOGO teaches programming through "Body-Syntonic
Geometry": you control an on-screen graphical "Turtle" holding a pen. By giving the
turtle simple directions from its own perspective ("step forward", "turn right"),
you can create intricate geometric patterns, art, and recursive fractals.

BasinPy includes a dedicated, high-speed Sinclair LOGO engine capable of running
both interactively and directly embedded inside Sinclair BASIC programs!

33.1 Launching LOGO in BasinPy
------------------------------
  - Interactive LOGO Shell:
      Type `LOGO` at the BASIC prompt. The screen clears and displays the turtle
      cursor (a small triangle) centered at HOME.
      Type `BYE`, `QUIT`, or `BASIC` to return to Sinclair BASIC.
  - Embedded LOGO in Sinclair BASIC:
      ```basic
      10 CLS
      20 LOGO
      30   CS
      40   SETPC 6
      50   REPEAT 4 [ FD 50 RT 90 ]
      60 END LOGO
      70 PRINT "Square complete!"
      ```

33.2 The Turtle and the Coordinate Space
----------------------------------------
  - The Turtle's Home:
      The turtle starts at the center of the Spectrum display:
      * Position: (x = 0, y = 0), which corresponds to screen pixel (128, 96).
      * Heading : 0 degrees (pointing straight North / Up toward the top of the CRT).
  - Screen Limits:
      * X-Axis: -128 (left border) to +127 (right border).
      * Y-Axis: -96 (bottom border) to +95 (top border).

33.3 Core Movement and Turning Commands
---------------------------------------
Every command has a standard full name and a convenient 2-letter abbreviation:

  - `FORWARD distance` / `FD d`:
      Moves the turtle forward `d` pixels in the direction it is currently facing.
      If the pen is down, it leaves a crisp line behind it.
      Example: `FD 50`

  - `BACK distance` / `BK d`:
      Moves the turtle backwards `d` pixels without changing its heading.
      Example: `BK 30`

  - `RIGHT angle` / `RT a`:
      Turns the turtle clockwise by `a` degrees.
      Example: `RT 90` (turns right by a quarter turn)

  - `LEFT angle` / `LT a`:
      Turns the turtle counter-clockwise by `a` degrees.
      Example: `LT 45` (turns left by an eighth turn)

  - `HOME`:
      Moves the turtle back to (0, 0) and sets its heading back to 0 (North).

  - `CLEARSCREEN` / `CS`:
      Clears the entire graphics canvas and homes the turtle.

33.4 Pen, Color, and Screen Controls
------------------------------------
  - `PENUP` / `PU`:
      Lifts the turtle's pen. Subsequent movements will NOT draw any lines.
  - `PENDOWN` / `PD`:
      Lowers the pen back to the paper so movement draws lines again.
  - `HIDETURTLE` / `HT`:
      Hides the turtle cursor. Your drawings render faster and look cleaner without
      the triangular pointer visible.
  - `SHOWTURTLE` / `ST`:
      Reveals the turtle cursor.
  - `SETPENCOLOR color` / `SETPC c`:
      Sets the turtle's drawing ink color (0..7):
      0: Black   1: Blue   2: Red   3: Magenta   4: Green   5: Cyan   6: Yellow   7: White
  - `SETBG color`:
      Sets the screen background (PAPER) color (0..7).
  - `BORDER color`:
      Sets the Sinclair outer border color (0..7).

33.5 Loops and Geometric Shapes (`REPEAT`)
------------------------------------------
The `REPEAT` command runs a block of bracketed commands multiple times:
  - Square (4 equal sides and four 90-degree turns):
      `REPEAT 4 [ FD 50 RT 90 ]`
  - Equilateral Triangle (3 sides, three 120-degree turns):
      `REPEAT 3 [ FD 60 RT 120 ]`
  - Regular Pentagon (5 sides, 72-degree turns: 360 / 5 = 72):
      `REPEAT 5 [ FD 40 RT 72 ]`
  - Smooth Circle (36 small steps and turns):
      `REPEAT 36 [ FD 5 RT 10 ]`
  - Brilliant 5-Point Star:
      `REPEAT 5 [ FD 80 RT 144 ]`

33.6 Defining Procedures with Parameters (`TO` and `END`)
---------------------------------------------------------
Just like Forth words or BASIC subroutines, LOGO allows you to define reusable
procedures using `TO <name>` and `END`:

  - Defining a Square of any size:
      `TO SQUARE :SIZE`
      `  REPEAT 4 [ FD :SIZE RT 90 ]`
      `END`
      Now you can call: `SQUARE 20`, `SQUARE 60`, `SQUARE 100`!

  - Creating Geometric Pinwheels:
      `TO PINWHEEL :COUNT :SIZE`
      `  REPEAT :COUNT [`
      `    SQUARE :SIZE`
      `    RT 360 / :COUNT`
      `  ]`
      `END`
      Calling `PINWHEEL 12 50` creates a gorgeous 12-sided geometric rosette!

33.7 Advanced Turtle Geometry: Recursion & Fractals
---------------------------------------------------
Because LOGO supports procedural recursion (a procedure calling itself with
smaller arguments), it is the ideal environment for generating stunning fractal art:

  - The Recursive Tree:
      Enter `LOGO` and type:
      ```logo
      TO TREE :LEN
        IF :LEN < 6 [ STOP ]
        FD :LEN
        LT 25
        TREE :LEN * 0.7
        RT 50
        TREE :LEN * 0.7
        LT 25
        BK :LEN
      END
      ```
      Now set colors and draw:
        `CS PU SETY -80 PD SETPC 4`
        `TREE 50`
      Watch a realistic organic tree branch out before your eyes!

  - The Infinite Spiral:
      ```logo
      TO SPIRAL :LEN :ANGLE
        IF :LEN > 120 [ STOP ]
        FD :LEN
        RT :ANGLE
        SPIRAL :LEN + 3 :ANGLE
      END
      ```
      Execute: `CS SETPC 6 SPIRAL 1 91`
      A hypnotic spiraling tunnel unfolds across the Spectrum screen!
      Type `BYE` to return cleanly to Sinclair BASIC.


================================================================================
 35. SECTION 34: STRUCTURED BASIC BEGINNER'S & REFERENCE GUIDE (BETA BASIC 3.0)
================================================================================

Standard 1982 Sinclair ZX Spectrum BASIC was a triumph of accessible personal
computing. However, as programs grew in size, developers ran directly into the
limitations of unstructured 8-bit BASIC:
  - The "Spaghetti Code" Problem: Unconstrained `GOTO` statements tangled code into
    an unreadable mess of line numbers.
  - Global Variable Clashes: Every variable in standard Sinclair BASIC is global. A
    loop variable `i` in a subroutine could accidentally overwrite `i` in your main
    game loop, causing mysterious, game-breaking bugs.
  - Subroutines Without Names: Calling `GO SUB 5000` gave no clue what the routine
    actually did, and renumbering programs was fraught with danger.

In 1983, Dr. Andy Wright created **Beta BASIC**, a groundbreaking software enhancement
that transformed the humble ZX Spectrum into a modern structured programming powerhouse.
Beta BASIC 3.0 introduced true named procedures with local parameter scoping, modern
looping blocks (`DO ... LOOP`, `WHILE ... WEND`, `REPEAT ... UNTIL`), multi-way `CASE`
branching, virtual scrolling windows, fast flood fills, and powerful editing tools.

BasinPy natively implements the complete Beta BASIC 3.0 language specification. All
Beta BASIC 3.0 keywords are highlighted in crisp Forest Green (`BB3`) in the editor
and execute directly inside the interpreter!

34.1 Named Procedures & Local Variable Scoping (`DEF PROC` & `LOCAL`)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Instead of anonymous `GO SUB 2000`, Beta BASIC allows you to define named, modular
procedures with private variables that cannot corrupt the rest of your program.

  - Procedure Definition Syntax:
      `DEF PROC ProcedureName(param1, param2, ...)`
        `LOCAL var1, var2, ...`
        `... statements ...`
      `END PROC`

  - Invoking Procedures:
      You can call a procedure using `PROC Name(args)` or simply by its name:
      `100 PROC DrawHealthBar(10, 20, 85)`
      `110 DrawHealthBar(10, 20, 85)`      ; Direct procedure call without PROC prefix!

  - The Power of `LOCAL`:
      Variables declared with `LOCAL` exist only while the procedure is actively
      running. When the procedure finishes at `END PROC`, the local variables are
      completely erased from memory!
      ```basic
      10 LET x = 999: LET y = 888
      20 PROC MoveAlien(10, 20)
      30 PRINT "Main x is still: "; x; " and y is: "; y  ; Prints 999 and 888!
      40 STOP
      50 DEF PROC MoveAlien(target_x, target_y)
      60   LOCAL x, y, step_count
      70   FOR x = 1 TO target_x
      80     LET y = x * 2
      90   NEXT x
      100 END PROC
      ```

  - Default Parameter Values (`DEFAULT`):
      If a caller omits an argument, `DEFAULT` provides a sensible fallback value:
      ```basic
      10 DEF PROC SetSpeed(spd)
      20   DEFAULT spd = 5
      30   PRINT "Current speed: "; spd
      40 END PROC
      ```
      Calling `PROC SetSpeed(10)` sets speed to 10; calling `PROC SetSpeed()` defaults to 5.

  - Passing Variables by Reference (`REF`):
      By default, arguments are passed by value. Prefixing a parameter with `REF`
      allows a procedure to directly alter the caller's variable:
      ```basic
      10 DEF PROC SwapCoords(REF a, REF b)
      20   LOCAL temp
      30   LET temp = a: LET a = b: LET b = temp
      40 END PROC
      ```

  - Early Exit (`EXIT PROC`):
      Immediately terminates procedure execution and returns control to the caller.

34.2 Modern Looping Structures: Escaping Spaghetti Code
-------------------------------------------------------
Beta BASIC 3.0 provides modern, elegant looping structures that make `GOTO` obsolete:

  1. Indefinite Loop with `EXIT` (`DO ... LOOP`):
       ```basic
       10 DO
       20   LET k$ = INKEY$
       30   IF k$ = "q" THEN EXIT
       40   IF k$ <> "" THEN PRINT "Key pressed: "; k$
       50 LOOP
       60 PRINT "Exited cleanly without GOTO!"
       ```
       `EXIT` breaks immediately out of the innermost enclosing loop!

  2. Pre-Conditioned Loops:
       - `DO WHILE condition ... LOOP`:
           ```basic
           10 LET energy = 100
           20 DO WHILE energy > 0
           30   PRINT "Shield holding at "; energy; "%"
           40   LET energy = energy - 15
           50 LOOP
           ```
       - `WHILE condition ... WEND`:
           Classic while-loop matching Pascal and modern BASICs.

  3. Post-Conditioned Loops:
       - `DO ... LOOP UNTIL condition`:
           Runs at least once, testing the condition at the bottom.
       - `REPEAT ... UNTIL condition`:
           ```basic
           10 REPEAT
           20   PRINT "Press 'Y' to confirm: ";
           30   PAUSE 0
           40   LET ans$ = INKEY$
           50 UNTIL ans$ = "y" OR ans$ = "Y"
           ```

34.3 Multi-Way Branching: `CASE ... OF ... ENDCASE`
---------------------------------------------------
Instead of clumsy cascades of `IF ... THEN IF ... THEN`, Beta BASIC introduces the
clean `CASE` statement:
```basic
100 PRINT "1. Start Game": PRINT "2. Instructions": PRINT "3. Quit"
110 INPUT "Choose option (1-3): ", opt$
120 CASE opt$ OF
130   WHEN "1": PROC StartGame
140   WHEN "2": PROC ShowHelp
150   WHEN "3": PRINT "Goodbye!": STOP
160   OTHERWISE: PRINT "Invalid choice! Please choose 1, 2, or 3."
170 ENDCASE
```
`WHEN` can take multiple comma-separated values: `WHEN "y", "Y", "yes": PROC Confirm`.

34.4 Virtual Windows, Graphics, and Smooth Scrolling
----------------------------------------------------
Beta BASIC 3.0 turns the Spectrum screen into a professional multi-window canvas:

  - `WINDOW x, y, width, height`:
      Restricts all subsequent `PRINT`, `CLS`, and cursor movements to a rectangular
      sub-area of the screen!
      ```basic
      10 CLS
      20 WINDOW 2, 5, 20, 10      ; Define a 20-column by 10-row window at row 2, col 5
      30 PAPER 1: INK 7: CLS      ; Clears ONLY the window area to Blue paper!
      40 FOR i = 1 TO 30
      50   PRINT "Line "; i       ; Text scrolls smoothly INSIDE the window!
      60 NEXT i
      70 WINDOW 0, 0, 32, 24      ; Reset window to full 32x24 screen
      ```

  - `BOX x, y, width, height [ATTR]`:
      Draws crisp geometric boxes on screen. `CLEAR BOX` clears the interior, and
      `FILL BOX` paints a solid color.

  - `FILL [x, y]`:
      High-speed machine code flood fill algorithm. Fills any bounded graphic
      contour with solid ink or hatching patterns!

  - `ROLL n` and `SCROLL n`:
      Smoothly scrolls the screen (or the active `WINDOW`) in any direction:
      up, down, left, or right, pixel-by-pixel or line-by-line.

  - `CSIZE width, height`:
      Dynamically reconfigures character rendering dimensions, allowing 64-column
      condensed text or double-height headlines.

34.5 Extended Functions and 16-Bit Memory Pointers
--------------------------------------------------
  - `DPOKE addr, val16` and `DPEEK(addr)`:
      Read and write 16-bit words directly, eliminating the tedious
      `POKE a, val AND 255: POKE a+1, INT(val/256)`.
  - Extended String Slicing:
      In standard Sinclair BASIC, `a$(5 TO 10)` crashes if `a$` is only 4 characters long.
      Beta BASIC features robust slicing: `a$(5 TO 10)` safely returns empty string `""`.
  - `STRING$(count, char$)` and `INSTR(start, text$, search$)`.
  - `TIME$` and `CLOCK`: Built-in real-time clock tracking hours, minutes, and seconds!

34.6 Program Editing and Refactoring Tools
------------------------------------------
Beta BASIC includes indispensable developer utilities for managing large codebases:
  - `RENUMBER [start, step]` (or `RENUM`): Renumbers the entire program and updates all
    `GOTO`, `GOSUB`, `RESTORE`, and `RUN` targets automatically.
  - `ALTER`: Interactive search and edit mode. Jumps directly to lines containing a query.
  - `FIND "target"`: Searches program lines for occurrences of target text.
  - `REPLACE "old" WITH "new"`: Global refactoring across all program lines.
  - `JOIN line1, line2`: Merges two program lines into one line separated by colons.
  - `SPLIT line, col`: Splits a complex program line into two separate numbered lines.
  - `TRON` and `TROFF`: Real-time execution tracing. `TRON` highlights currently executing
    line numbers on screen, making logic bugs instantly obvious!

34.7 Hands-On Case Study: Modular Dungeon Explorer
--------------------------------------------------
Here is a complete, structured mini-game demonstrating procedures, local variables,
`DO ... LOOP`, `CASE`, and virtual windows in BasinPy:
```basic
10 CLEAR 32767
20 CLS
30 PROC GameInit
40 PROC MainLoop
50 STOP
60 
70 DEF PROC GameInit
80   GLOBAL p_x, p_y, gold, dungeon_active
90   LET p_x = 5: LET p_y = 5: LET gold = 0: LET dungeon_active = 1
100   BORDER 0: PAPER 0: INK 7: CLS
110   WINDOW 1, 1, 30, 20
120   BOX 0, 0, 30, 20
130   PRINT AT 0, 8; "[ DUNGEON EXPLORER ]"
140 END PROC
150 
160 DEF PROC MainLoop
170   DO WHILE dungeon_active
180     PROC DrawStatus
190     PROC GetPlayerMove
200     IF gold >= 50 THEN
210       PRINT AT 10, 5; "YOU ESCAPED WITH THE TREASURE!"
220       LET dungeon_active = 0
230     END IF
240   LOOP
250 END PROC
260 
270 DEF PROC DrawStatus
280   PRINT AT 18, 2; "X: "; p_x; " Y: "; p_y; " Gold: "; gold; " GP   "
290 END PROC
300 
310 DEF PROC GetPlayerMove
320   LOCAL k$
330   PRINT AT 19, 2; "Move (N, S, E, W) or (Q)uit: ";
340   DO
350     LET k$ = INKEY$
360     IF k$ <> "" THEN EXIT
370   LOOP
380   PRINT k$
390   CASE k$ OF
400     WHEN "n", "N": LET p_y = p_y - 1: PROC CheckRoom
410     WHEN "s", "S": LET p_y = p_y + 1: PROC CheckRoom
420     WHEN "e", "E": LET p_x = p_x + 1: PROC CheckRoom
430     WHEN "w", "W": LET p_x = p_x - 1: PROC CheckRoom
440     WHEN "q", "Q": LET dungeon_active = 0
450     OTHERWISE: PRINT AT 17, 2; "Invalid key! Use N, S, E, W    "
460   ENDCASE
470 END PROC
480 
490 DEF PROC CheckRoom
500   LOCAL found
510   LET found = INT (RND * 20) + 5
520   LET gold = gold + found
530   PRINT AT 15, 2; "Found a chest with "; found; " gold coins!   "
540 END PROC
```
Run this game in BasinPy (`RUN`) to see structured Beta BASIC in action! Notice how clean,
readable, and modular the code is—completely free of spaghetti jumps.



================================================================================
 36. SECTION 35: CP/M 2.2 & CP/M PLUS (v3.0) USER GUIDE (SPECTRUM +3 ENVIRONMENT)
================================================================================

35.1 What is CP/M? (The Industry-Standard 8-Bit Operating System)
-----------------------------------------------------------------
In the history of personal computing, few operating systems hold as legendary a status
as CP/M (Control Program for Microcomputers). Developed by Gary Kildall and Digital
Research in 1974, CP/M became the universal business and professional software standard
for Intel 8080 and Zilog Z80 microcomputers throughout the late 1970s and 1980s.
Before MS-DOS and the IBM PC, CP/M was the standard platform that ran the world's
business spreadsheets, word processors, databases, and programming languages.

When Amstrad acquired Sinclair Research in 1986, they sought to bridge the gap between
home gaming computers and serious office productivity. In 1987, Amstrad released the
Sinclair ZX Spectrum +3, featuring 128K banked RAM, an integrated 3-inch floppy disk drive,
and an advanced disk operating system. Amstrad commissioned Locomotive Software (creators
of Locomotive BASIC and the Amstrad PCW system software) to port CP/M Plus (CP/M 3.0)
to the Spectrum +3.

CP/M Plus on the Spectrum +3 was remarkable:
  - Full Backward Compatibility: It ran standard CP/M 2.2 software without modification.
  - Generous Transient Program Area: By utilizing the Spectrum +3's banked memory, it
    provided a massive 61K TPA (Transient Program Area: 0x0100 to 0xFD00) for running
    demanding business applications.
  - High-Density Screen Modes: The +3 introduced custom high-density 51-column text
    display modes using a specialized 5x8 font, bringing professional text formatting
    to the standard television set or CRT monitor.

BasinPy features a complete, native implementation of the Amstrad/Sinclair ZX Spectrum +3
CP/M Plus (CP/M 2.2 compatible) environment. It provides full Console Command Processor
(CCP) interpretation, BDOS system call vectoring (traps at 0x0005 and 0xFDF0), BIOS jump
tables, multi-drive virtual floppy support, native Z80 binary execution, and comprehensive
terminal emulation (VT52, Lear Siegler ADM-3A, and VT100 / ANSI).


35.2 Entering and Navigating CP/M in BasinPy (CPM, SPEC, Drives A:-P:)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
BasinPy makes transitioning between the Sinclair ZX Spectrum environment and CP/M
completely seamless.

1. Entering CP/M:
   From Sinclair BASIC (or the immediate command prompt), enter CP/M using any of the
   following methods:
     - `CPM`
         Enters CP/M mode directly in the current window.
     - `CPM /FS`
         Enters CP/M mode in full-screen authentic green/amber CRT presentation.
     - `LOAD "plus3_cpm3.dsk"`  or  `ATTACH "plus3_cpm3.dsk"`
         Mounts the official Spectrum +3 CP/M system disk and boots directly into CP/M.
     - `CPM "COMMAND"`
         Executes a CP/M command directly from Sinclair BASIC (e.g. `CPM "DIR"` or
         `CPM "MALLARD"`).

   Upon entry, CP/M displays its authentic startup banner:
     ```
     Amstrad/Sinclair +3 CP/M Plus v3.0 (2.2 compatible)
     Copyright (C) 1987 Locomotive Software / Digital Research
     61K TPA, BIOS v1.1
     Drive A: mounted "plus3_cpm3.dsk" (Sinclair +3 / PCW 180K (CF2))
     Display: 51x24 default (+3 font). Commands: DISPLAY 32|51|80, SET24X80

     A>
     ```

2. The Command Prompt and Drive Navigation:
   The `A>` prompt indicates that Drive A: is the currently logged disk drive.
   To switch active drives, type the target drive letter followed by a colon:
     - `B:`     Switches the logged drive to Drive B: (prompt becomes `B>`).
     - `A:`     Switches back to Drive A:.
     - `M:`     Switches to the high-speed RAM Disk (prompt becomes `M>`).

   Drives supported in BasinPy:
     - `A:`     Primary floppy drive (Spectrum +3 internal 3" drive: 180K or 720K).
     - `B:`     Secondary floppy drive (external 3" or 3.5" drive: 180K, 360K, 720K).
     - `M:`     RAM Disk (64K fast silicon memory for temporary work and compilation).
     - `C:`-`P:` Logical CP/M drives for mounted foreign disks and virtual partitions.

3. Returning to Sinclair BASIC:
   To exit CP/M and return to Sinclair BASIC at any time:
     - Type `SPEC` at any CCP prompt:
         `A> SPEC`
     - BasinPy cleanly flushes and syncs all mounted disk images to disk, restores the
       standard Sinclair 48K/128K 32x24 display, resets colors and fonts, and greets
       you with the classic report code:
         `0 OK, 0:1`

4. Screen Display Modes (`DISPLAY` and `SET24X80`):
   BasinPy's CP/M subsystem supports three distinct display modes to suit any application:
     - `DISPLAY 51` (Default):
         Authentic Sinclair +3 51-column by 24-row mode using the proprietary +3 5x8
         compact font. Perfect for WordStar, Mallard BASIC, and CP/M utilities on standard
         displays.
     - `DISPLAY 32`:
         Sinclair ROM 32-column display. Ideal for retro aesthetics and low-resolution viewing.
     - `DISPLAY 80` (or run `SET24X80`):
         Full 80-column by 24-row professional terminal display emulation. Opens a dedicated
         80x24 VT100 / ANSI terminal window capable of running full-width business spreadsheets
         and database software.


35.3 Built-in CCP Commands Reference (DIR, ERA, REN, TYPE, USER, SAVE)
----------------------------------------------------------------------
The Console Command Processor (CCP) is the command-line interpreter of CP/M. The following
commands are built directly into memory and execute instantaneously without accessing the disk:

1. `DIR [filespec]` - Directory Listing
   Lists files stored on the currently logged drive or a specified drive.
   - Syntax:
       `DIR`                  (Lists all files on the current drive)
       `DIR B:`               (Lists all files on Drive B:)
       `DIR *.BAS`            (Lists all files with extension .BAS)
       `DIR W*.*`             (Lists all files starting with letter W)
   - Output format: Files are displayed in multi-column format with drive prefix and 8.3 name:
       `A: PIP      COM : STAT     COM : MALLARD  COM`
       `A: WORDSTAR COM : CALCSTAR COM : DATASTAR COM`
   - If no files match the search pattern, CP/M responds with: `No File`.

2. `ERA filespec` - Erase / Delete Files
   Deletes one or more files from disk.
   - Syntax:
       `ERA TEST.DAT`         (Deletes TEST.DAT from the logged drive)
       `ERA B:*.BAK`          (Deletes all backup files from Drive B:)
       `ERA *.*`              (Erases all files in the current user area)
   - Note: If a file is marked with the Read-Only (`R/O`) attribute, CP/M protects it and
     reports `FILENAME is R/O`. The attribute must be changed to `R/W` using `STAT` before
     erasure is permitted.

3. `REN newname=oldname` - Rename File
   Renames an existing file on disk.
   - Syntax:
       `REN NEWPROG.BAS=OLDPROG.BAS`
       `REN B:BACKUP.TXT=B:REPORT.TXT`
   - If the old file does not exist, CP/M reports `No File`.
   - If a file with the new name already exists, CP/M reports `File Exists` to prevent
     accidental overwriting.

4. `TYPE filespec` - Display Text File
   Displays the ASCII contents of a text file on the screen.
   - Syntax:
       `TYPE README.TXT`
       `TYPE B:WELCOME.WS`
   - CP/M files traditionally terminate with a `Control-Z` (ASCII 26 / 0x1A) end-of-file
     marker. `TYPE` streams characters to the display and cleanly stops upon encountering
     the `^Z` byte.
   - Pausing output: Press `Space` or `Control-S` to pause scrolling; press any key to resume.
     Press `Control-C` to abort output and return to the CCP prompt.

5. `USER n` - Switch User Area (0-15)
   CP/M supports 16 logical "User Areas" (0 through 15) on each drive, allowing multiple
   directories of files to share the same physical disk without collision.
   - Syntax:
       `USER 0`               (Switch to default User Area 0)
       `USER 1`               (Switch to User Area 1)
       `USER`                 (Displays the currently active user number)
   - Files created in User 1 are invisible from User 0 and vice versa, providing clean
     project organization.

6. `SAVE n filespec` - Save Memory Pages to Disk
   Saves `n` memory pages (each page = 256 bytes) starting from the base of the Transient
   Program Area (0x0100) to a disk file.
   - Syntax:
       `SAVE 2 MYPROG.COM`    (Saves 512 bytes: 2 pages * 256 bytes from 0x0100 to 0x02FF)
   - Used extensively after debugging with `DDT` or entering machine code patches to produce
     executable `.COM` program files.

7. `CLS` - Clear Screen
   Clears the CP/M screen and positions the cursor at row 0, column 0.

8. `HELP` - Built-in Quick Reference
   Displays an overview of available CCP commands, transient utilities, and instructions
   for returning to Sinclair BASIC (`SPEC`).


35.4 Standard CP/M Transient Utilities (STAT, PIP, ED, DDT, SUBMIT)
-------------------------------------------------------------------
Unlike built-in commands, "Transient Programs" are utility programs loaded from disk files
(with extension `.COM`) into the TPA at address 0x0100. BasinPy includes the full suite
of authentic CP/M utilities:

1. `STAT` - Disk and File Status Utility
   `STAT` provides detailed telemetry on drive usage, file attributes, and physical device
   assignments:
   - Check free disk space:
       `A> STAT`
       Output:
       `A: R/W, Space: 142k`
       `B: R/W, Space: 180k`
       `M: R/W, Space: 64k`
   - Check file sizes and allocation extents:
       `A> STAT *.*`
       Displays size in KB, 128-byte records, byte count, extents, and access status:
       ```
       Size  Recs  Bytes  Ext Acc
         4k    32   3840    1 R/W A:MALLARD.COM
        12k    96  12288    1 R/W A:WORDSTAR.COM
         8k    64   8192    1 R/O A:SYSTEM.DAT
       ```
   - Change file access attributes:
       `STAT MYFILE.BAS $R/O`     (Marks file Read-Only: prevents deletion and overwrites)
       `STAT MYFILE.BAS $R/W`     (Restores file to normal Read/Write status)
   - Inspect logical device assignments:
       `STAT DEV:`  or  `STAT VAL:`
       Displays assignments for console (`CON:`), line printer (`LST:`), punch (`PUN:`),
       and reader (`RDR:`).

2. `PIP` - Peripheral Interchange Program
   `PIP` is the universal CP/M file copying and device transfer utility.
   - Syntax:
       `PIP destination=source`
   - Copy a file from Drive A: to Drive B::
       `PIP B:=A:WELCOME.TXT`
   - Copy all BASIC files from Drive A: to Drive B::
       `PIP B:=A:*.BAS`
   - Rename while copying:
       `PIP B:NEWNAME.DAT=A:OLDNAME.DAT`
   - Interactive PIP Mode:
       Type `PIP` without arguments to enter the interactive prompt (`*`).
       Enter copy commands one after another:
         `* B:=A:*.COM`
         `* B:=A:*.BAS`
         `* [Press Enter to exit PIP and return to CCP]`

3. `ED` - Character and Line Text Editor
   `ED` is the standard CP/M text editor used to create and modify program source code,
   batch scripts, and data files.
   - Launching:
       `ED TEST.SUB`
   - The editor loads the file into memory and presents the `*` command prompt.
   - Core ED commands:
       `#A`         Read all lines into memory buffer.
       `T`          Type (display) the current line.
       `5T`         Type the next 5 lines.
       `I`          Enter Insert Mode (type text; press `Control-Z` to exit Insert Mode).
       `D`          Delete current character.
       `K`          Kill (delete) current line.
       `B`          Move pointer to Beginning of buffer.
       `-B`         Move pointer to End of buffer.
       `E`          End editing: save buffer to disk and exit to CP/M.
       `Q`          Quit without saving changes.

4. `DDT` - Dynamic Debugging Tool
   `DDT` is the interactive Z80 machine code monitor and debugger for CP/M.
   - Launching:
       `DDT`               (Starts debugger with empty TPA)
       `DDT PROGRAM.COM`   (Loads PROGRAM.COM into memory starting at 0x0100 for debugging)
   - Prompt: Presents the `-` prompt.
   - Core DDT commands:
       `D [start][,end]`   Display memory hex and ASCII dump (e.g. `D 0100,0140`).
       `L [start][,end]`   Disassemble memory as Z80 mnemonics (e.g. `L 0100`).
       `A address`         Inline assembler: type Z80 assembly instructions directly into memory!
       `T [n]`             Trace execution: single-step `n` instructions, printing register state.
       `U [n]`             Untagged trace: step through code without tracing CALL subroutines.
       `G [start][,stop]`  Go: execute code starting at `start` until breakpoint `stop`.
       `X [register]`      Examine and modify CPU registers (e.g. `X`, `XA`, `XPC`, `XSP`).
       `Control-C`         Exit DDT and return to CCP.

5. `DUMP filespec` - Binary File Hex Dumper
   Displays a formatted 16-byte-per-line hexadecimal and ASCII dump of any disk file:
     `A> DUMP MALLARD.COM`
     Output:
     ```
     0000 31 00 FD CD 20 02 21 00 01 11 00 FD 01 00 FC ED  1... .!.........
     0010 B0 C3 00 01 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00  ................
     ```

6. `SUBMIT filename [parameters...]` - Batch Command Automation
   Executes automated batch scripts containing sequences of CP/M commands.
   - Create a script `BUILD.SUB`:
       ```text
       ; Automated build script
       MALLARD $1.BAS
       STAT $1.*
       DIR B:
       ```
   - Run the script:
       `SUBMIT BUILD TEST`
   - `SUBMIT` automatically substitutes `$1` with `TEST`, executes each command in order,
     and returns cleanly to the prompt when finished.

7. `DISCKIT` - Spectrum +3 Floppy Management Utility
   Authentic port of the Sinclair +3 floppy disk maintenance tool.
   - Launch: `DISCKIT`
   - Menu options:
       1. Format Disk (Initializes Drive A: or B: with fresh 0xE5 sectors and directory).
       2. Copy Disk (Fast track-by-track sector copy).
       3. Verify Disk (Scans tracks for CRC and sector integrity).
       4. Quit to CP/M.


35.5 Running the Microstar Professional Application Suite (WordStar, CalcStar, DataStar)
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
BasinPy includes built-in implementations of the legendary MicroPro business software suite,
fully integrated with the Spectrum +3 CP/M environment:

1. WordStar 3.0 / 2.0 (`WS.COM` or `WORDSTAR.COM`)
   The most widely used word processor of the 8-bit era. WordStar introduced touch-typing
   cursor control and WYSIWYG document preparation to personal computers.
   - Launching: Type `WS` or `WORDSTAR` (or `WS FILENAME.TXT` to open immediately).
   - Opening Menu (No-File Menu):
       `D`   Open a Document file (for formatted articles, letters, manuals).
       `N`   Open a Non-Document file (for programming source code and batch scripts).
       `L`   Change logged drive (switch between A:, B:, M:).
       `F`   Toggle file directory display on/off.
       `P`   Print a document file.
       `E`   Rename a file.
       `O`   Copy a file.
       `Y`   Delete a file.
       `X`   Exit WordStar and return to CP/M.
   - The WordStar Editing Screen:
       * Status Line: Shows current drive, filename, Page, Line, and Column position.
       * Ruler Line: Shows left margin (`L`), tab stops (`!`), and right margin (`R`).
       * Viewport: 21 lines of high-density 51-column text display.
   - The Famous WordStar Diamond Cursor Control:
       Keep your hands on the home row! Hold `Control` and press:
         `^E`  Cursor Up one line
         `^X`  Cursor Down one line
         `^S`  Cursor Left one character
         `^D`  Cursor Right one character
         `^A`  Word Left
         `^F`  Word Right
         `^R`  Page Up (Scroll up one screen)
         `^C`  Page Down (Scroll down one screen)
         `^W`  Scroll screen down one line without moving cursor
         `^Z`  Scroll screen up one line without moving cursor
   - Block Operations (`Control-K` Prefix):
         `^KB` Mark Beginning of block
         `^KK` Mark End of block
         `^KC` Copy marked block to current cursor position
         `^KV` Move marked block to current cursor position
         `^KY` Delete marked block
         `^KW` Write block to external file
         `^KR` Read external file and insert at cursor
   - Formatting and Margins (`Control-O` Prefix):
         `^OC` Center current line between margins
         `^OW` Toggle word wrap on/off
         `^OL` Set Left Margin column
         `^OR` Set Right Margin column
         `^B`  Reform paragraph: re-aligns text to current margins from cursor to blank line.
   - Saving and Exiting (`Control-K` Prefix):
         `^KS` Save document and continue editing
         `^KD` Save document and return to WordStar Opening Menu
         `^KX` Save document and exit directly to CP/M
         `^KQ` Abandon document (quit without saving edits)
   - Dot Commands: Lines beginning with a period in column 1 control print formatting:
         `.he Page Header Text`
         `.fo Page Footer Text`
         `.pa` (Forces a page break)
         `.op` (Omit page numbering)

2. CalcStar 1.4 (`CS.COM` or `CALCSTAR.COM`)
   The professional 26-column by 255-row electronic spreadsheet for CP/M.
   - Launching: Type `CS` or `CALCSTAR` (or `CS BUDGET.CAL`).
   - Grid Coordinates: Columns labeled A through Z; rows numbered 1 through 255.
   - Entering Data: Move cursor to desired cell and type text labels or numbers.
   - Mathematical Formulas: Begin formula with `+` or `=` (e.g. `+A1+B1` or `+A1*1.15`).
   - Built-in Functions:
       `SUM(range)`      Sum of cells in range (e.g. `SUM(B1:B12)`)
       `AVG(range)`      Arithmetic average of cells
       `CNT(range)`      Count of non-empty cells
       `MIN(range)`      Minimum value in range
       `MAX(range)`      Maximum value in range
       `ABS(expr)`       Absolute value
       `INT(expr)`       Truncate to integer
       `SQRT(expr)`      Square root
       `ROUND(expr, n)`  Round to `n` decimal places
       `IF(cond, v1, v2)`Conditional branch
   - Slash (`/`) Commands:
       `/F`   Format cell: Currency (`$`), Fixed 2 decimals (`F2`), Integer (`I`), Bar chart (`*`).
       `/C`   Copy cell or range to another location with automatic relative coordinate update.
       `/M`   Move cell or row/column.
       `/D`   Delete row or column.
       `/I`   Insert blank row or column.
       `/W`   Set column display width (e.g. 12 characters).
       `/B`   Blank / erase cell contents.
       `/S`   Save spreadsheet to `.CAL` file.
       `/L`   Load spreadsheet from `.CAL` file.
       `/P`   Print spreadsheet grid.
       `/R`   Recalculate all formulas.
       `/Q`   Quit CalcStar and return to CP/M.

3. DataStar (`DS.COM` or `DATASTAR.COM`)
   Visual database management and screen forms data entry system.
   - Launching: Type `DS` or `DATASTAR` (e.g. `DS CONTACTS.DTA`).
   - Field Definitions: Supports structured fields with validation rules:
       * Alphanumeric (`A`): Text names, addresses, descriptions.
       * Numeric (`N`): Integer and floating point quantities.
       * Currency (`$`): Monetary amounts with automatic formatting.
       * Calculated (`C`): Real-time evaluation of mathematical expressions
         (e.g. `AVAIL = LIMIT - BALANCE`).
       * Primary Key: Unique index field for rapid indexed search.
       * Required: Prevents saving records with blank essential data.
   - Operating Modes:
       * Form View: Clean, full-screen graphical form display for viewing and editing records.
       * Tabular List / Browse: Multi-row spreadsheet-style overview of all records.
       * Seek / Search: Instant lookup by primary key or partial substring match.
   - Record Storage: Persisted in standard ASCII `.DTA` format with CRLF record delimiters,
     allowing seamless data export to CSV or import into Mallard BASIC.


35.6 Multi-Format Disk Mounting in BasinPy (11 Supported Floppy Formats)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
In the 1980s, the CP/M world was fragmented: almost every computer manufacturer utilized
their own proprietary floppy disk format. Moving disks between an Osborne, a Kaypro, and
a Sinclair +3 was historically a major challenge.

BasinPy solves this completely with its **Universal Multi-Format Disk Image Engine**.
BasinPy transparently inspects, identifies, reads, formats, and writes 11 industry-standard
CP/M floppy disk formats:

| Format ID        | Description & System               | Tracks | Sides | Sec/Trk | Sec Size | Capacity |
|------------------|------------------------------------|--------|-------|---------|----------|----------|
| `plus3_180k`     | Sinclair +3 / Amstrad PCW (CF2)    | 40     | 1     | 9       | 512      | 180 KB   |
| `plus3_720k`     | Sinclair +3 / Amstrad PCW (CF2DD)  | 80     | 2     | 9       | 512      | 720 KB   |
| `cpc_360k`       | Amstrad CPC / PCW (DSDD)           | 40     | 2     | 9       | 512      | 360 KB   |
| `ibm_3740_sssd`  | IBM 3740 Standard 8" SSSD          | 77     | 1     | 26      | 128      | 250 KB   |
| `kaypro_ii`      | Kaypro II Portable CP/M            | 40     | 1     | 10      | 512      | 200 KB   |
| `kaypro_4`       | Kaypro 4 Double-Sided              | 80     | 1     | 10      | 512      | 400 KB   |
| `osborne_sssd`   | Osborne 1 Single Density           | 40     | 1     | 10      | 256      | 100 KB   |
| `osborne_ssdd`   | Osborne 1 Double Density           | 40     | 1     | 5       | 1024     | 200 KB   |
| `apple2_softcard`| Apple II Microsoft SoftCard CP/M   | 35     | 1     | 16      | 256      | 140 KB   |
| `c128_1571`      | Commodore 128 (1571 Drive)         | 70     | 1     | 5       | 1024     | 340 KB   |
| `pc_720k`        | Generic PC 3.5" CP/M Format        | 80     | 2     | 9       | 512      | 720 KB   |

Mounting Disks in BasinPy:
  - From CP/M:
      `A> MOUNT "my_kaypro_disk.dsk", B`
      `A> ADD DISK "archive.img", B`
      `A> ATTACH "c128_software.dsk"`
  - From Sinclair BASIC:
      `LOAD "my_kaypro_disk.dsk"`
      `ATTACH "archive.img", 2`
  - Automatic Geometry Detection:
      BasinPy analyzes track layouts, sector sizes, directory block markers, and file
      extents. You never need to configure track or sector counts manually—BasinPy
      identifies the disk format and mounts it with full Read/Write capability immediately!


================================================================================
 37. SECTION 36: LOCOMOTIVE MALLARD-80 BASIC USER GUIDE & REFERENCE
================================================================================

36.1 Introduction to Mallard-80 BASIC on CP/M
---------------------------------------------
When Amstrad bundled CP/M Plus with the Sinclair ZX Spectrum +3, they included what is
widely recognized as one of the most sophisticated, robust, and lightning-fast 8-bit BASIC
interpreters ever created: **Locomotive Mallard-80 BASIC with Jet**.

Authored by Locomotive Software (the celebrated software house led by Richard Clayton and
Chris Hall), Mallard-80 BASIC was modeled on the Microsoft BASIC-80 (MBASIC) industry
standard while adding significant extensions:
  - Jet ISAM Engine: Integrated B-tree indexed sequential access method (ISAM) database
    capabilities for managing structured records on disk.
  - Multi-Precision Numeric Engine: Complete support for 16-bit integers, 64-bit single-precision
    floats, and 128-bit double-precision IEEE floats (providing up to 16 significant decimal
    digits of accuracy for accounting and scientific computation).
  - Advanced File Handling: Full sequential file access and byte-level random access with
    fixed-length record buffers (`FIELD`, `LSET`, `RSET`, `PUT`, `GET`).
  - Professional Formatting: Rich `PRINT USING` formatting specifiers with currency signs,
    comma separators, asterisk check protection, and fixed-width string masks.

In BasinPy, Mallard-80 BASIC is fully integrated into the CP/M subsystem. It can be launched
directly from CP/M, invoked from Sinclair BASIC, or used to run existing CP/M `.BAS` software.

How to Launch Mallard BASIC:
  - From the CP/M prompt (`A>`):
      `A> MALLARD`
      `A> BASIC`
      `A> BAS`
  - To load and execute a program immediately:
      `A> MALLARD INVOICE.BAS`
  - From Sinclair BASIC:
      `LOAD "BASIC.COM"`

Startup Banner:
  ```
  Mallard-80 BASIC with Jet Version 1.29
  (c) 1987 Locomotive Software Ltd
  43285 Bytes free
  Ok
  ```
The `Ok` prompt signals that Mallard BASIC is in Direct Command Mode and ready for your input.


36.2 Language Fundamentals & Syntax Differences from Sinclair BASIC
-------------------------------------------------------------------
While users familiar with Sinclair ZX Spectrum BASIC will recognize many core commands,
Mallard-80 BASIC follows the professional CP/M / MBASIC standard and possesses several
important structural differences:

1. Line Numbers and Multi-Statement Lines:
   - Line numbers range from 1 to 65529.
   - Multiple statements on a single line are separated by a colon (`:`):
       `10 CLS : PRINT "WELCOME TO MALLARD" : GOSUB 1000`

2. Explicit Data Typing via Variable Suffixes:
   Unlike Sinclair BASIC (where numeric variables are always 5-byte floating point), Mallard
   BASIC supports four distinct data types identified by trailing type suffixes:
     - `%`  Integer (16-bit signed: -32,768 to +32,767). Fast integer math for counters.
            Example: `count% = 100`
     - `!`  Single Precision (6 significant decimal digits). Default if no suffix is given.
            Example: `price! = 19.99`  or  `price = 19.99`
     - `#`  Double Precision (16 significant decimal digits). High precision for financial math.
            Example: `pi# = 3.141592653589793`
     - `$`  String (0 to 255 characters).
            Example: `customer$ = "Acme Industrial"`

3. Default Type Declarations (`DEFINT`, `DEFSNG`, `DEFDBL`, `DEFSTR`):
   You can establish default variable types across alphabetical ranges without needing suffixes:
     - `DEFINT I-N`     (Variables starting with I, J, K, L, M, N are automatically Integers)
     - `DEFDBL A-H`     (Variables starting with A through H are automatically Double Precision)
     - `DEFSTR S`       (Variables starting with S are automatically Strings)

4. Arithmetic and Logical Operators:
     - `\`  Integer Division: Truncates remainder (e.g. `7 \ 2` yields `3`).
     - `MOD` Modulo: Returns division remainder (e.g. `7 MOD 2` yields `1`).
     - `^`  Power / Exponentiation (e.g. `2 ^ 8` yields `256`).
     - Logical / Bitwise Operators: `AND`, `OR`, `XOR`, `NOT`, `EQV` (equivalence), `IMP` (implication).
     - Relational Operators: `=`, `<>`, `<`, `>`, `<=`, `>=`.

5. Sinclair Spectrum Keyword Isolation:
   In BasinPy, typing Sinclair-specific keywords (`BORDER`, `INK`, `PAPER`, `FLASH`, `BRIGHT`,
   `PLOT`, `DRAW`, `CIRCLE`) inside Mallard BASIC triggers an informative syntax message:
     `Syntax error (Spectrum keyword 'BORDER' not valid in CP/M Mallard BASIC)`
   This ensures complete isolation between the 1982 Sinclair ROM environment and the professional
   1987 CP/M business platform.


36.3 Program Editing Commands (AUTO, RENUM, DELETE, LIST, SAVE, LOAD)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Mallard BASIC provides a comprehensive suite of program entry and maintenance commands:

1. `AUTO [start][, increment]` - Automatic Line Numbering
   Automatically prompts with line numbers so you don't have to type them manually:
     `AUTO`           (Starts at line 10, increments by 10)
     `AUTO 100, 20`   (Starts at line 100, increments by 20)
   Press `Enter` on a blank line to terminate AUTO mode.

2. `RENUM [new_start][, old_start][, increment]` - Intelligent Program Renumbering
   Renumbers the lines of your program. Crucially, Mallard BASIC automatically updates all
   line references in `GOTO`, `GOSUB`, `THEN`, `ELSE`, `RESTORE`, and `RESUME` statements:
     `RENUM`          (Renumbers entire program to 10, 20, 30...)
     `RENUM 100,,10`  (Renumbers program starting at 100 with step 10)
     `RENUM 1000, 500, 10` (Renumbers lines from 500 onwards, starting at 1000)

3. `DELETE [start][-end]` - Erase Program Lines
   Deletes a single line or an entire range of program lines:
     `DELETE 50`      (Deletes line 50)
     `DELETE 100-200` (Deletes all lines from 100 to 200 inclusive)
     `DELETE -150`    (Deletes all lines from the start up to line 150)

4. `LIST [start][-end]` - List Program Code
   Displays program lines on the screen:
     `LIST`           (Lists entire program)
     `LIST 100`       (Displays line 100)
     `LIST 100-300`   (Displays lines 100 through 300)

5. `LLIST [start][-end]` - Print Listing to ZX Printer / PDF
   Sends the program listing to the printer. In BasinPy, `LLIST` connects directly to the
   high-resolution virtual ZX Printer and prompts you to save a publication-quality PDF
   document of your source code!

6. `NEW` - Clear Working Memory
   Erases the current program, clears all variables and arrays, and closes all open files.

7. `RUN [line]` - Execute Program
   Starts program execution from line 1 (or from a specified line number).

8. `STOP` and `CONT` - Program Break and Continuation
   - `STOP` pauses program execution and displays:
       `Break in line 150`
   - You can inspect variables at the direct prompt (e.g. `PRINT score%`).
   - Type `CONT` to resume program execution right where it stopped!

9. `TRON` and `TROFF` - Execution Trace
   - `TRON` (Trace On) prints line numbers as they execute (e.g. `[10][20][50]`), allowing
     you to visually follow program flow and find infinite loops.
   - `TROFF` (Trace Off) disables tracing.

10. Disk Storage (`SAVE`, `LOAD`, `MERGE`, `KILL`, `NAME`, `FILES`):
    - `SAVE "PROGRAM.BAS"`     Saves program to the active CP/M disk.
    - `LOAD "PROGRAM.BAS"`     Loads program from disk into memory.
    - `MERGE "ROUTINE.BAS"`    Merges lines from disk file into the current program.
    - `FILES`  or  `DIR`       Displays disk directory without exiting BASIC.
    - `KILL "TEMP.DAT"`        Deletes a file from disk.
    - `NAME "OLD.BAS" AS "NEW.BAS"` Renames a file on disk.


36.4 Structured Control Flow & Data Types (%, !, #, $, WHILE/WEND)
------------------------------------------------------------------
Mallard-80 BASIC provides structured programming constructs that eliminate the need for
unstructured `GOTO` jumps:

1. `WHILE ... WEND` - Modern Pre-Test Loop
   Repeats a block of statements as long as the condition remains true. The loop condition
   is tested before the loop body is executed:
   ```basic
   10 balance# = 1000.00
   20 month% = 0
   30 WHILE balance# > 0
   40   month% = month% + 1
   50   balance# = balance# - 85.50
   60   PRINT "Month:"; month%, "Remaining: £"; balance#
   70 WEND
   80 PRINT "Balance cleared after "; month%; " months."
   ```

2. `FOR ... TO ... STEP ... NEXT`
   Standard counted loop with optional step value:
   ```basic
   10 FOR i% = 10 TO 1 STEP -1
   20   PRINT i%; " seconds to launch..."
   30 NEXT i%
   40 PRINT "BLAST OFF!"
   ```

3. Multi-Line and Structured `IF ... THEN ... ELSE`
   Supports direct statements and multi-statement execution on both branches:
   ```basic
   100 IF age% >= 18 THEN PRINT "Adult" : fee% = 10 ELSE PRINT "Minor" : fee% = 5
   ```

4. `SWAP variable1, variable2` - Exchange Values Instantly
   Swaps the values of two variables of the same type without requiring a temporary third variable:
   ```basic
   10 a$ = "World" : b$ = "Hello"
   20 SWAP a$, b$
   30 PRINT a$; " "; b$    ' Prints: Hello World
   ```

5. `MID$` as a Statement (In-Place String Mutation)
   In Sinclair BASIC, modifying characters inside a string requires complex slicing concatenation.
   In Mallard BASIC, `MID$` can appear on the *left side* of an assignment statement to overwrite
   characters in-place:
   ```basic
   10 a$ = "Price: $00.00"
   20 MID$(a$, 9, 5) = "19.95"
   30 PRINT a$            ' Prints: Price: $19.95
   ```

6. Computed Branching (`ON ... GOTO` and `ON ... GOSUB`):
   ```basic
   10 PRINT "1. Load  2. Save  3. Quit"
   20 INPUT "Choice (1-3): "; choice%
   30 ON choice% GOSUB 1000, 2000, 3000
   ```

7. Error Trapping (`ON ERROR GOTO`, `RESUME`, `ERR`, `ERL`):
   Mallard BASIC provides true structured exception handling:
   ```basic
   10 ON ERROR GOTO 9000
   20 INPUT "Enter filename: "; f$
   30 OPEN "I", #1, f$
   40 ' Read file...
   50 CLOSE #1
   60 END
   9000 ' Error recovery routine
   9010 PRINT "Disk Error #"; ERR; " occurred in line "; ERL
   9020 IF ERR = 53 THEN PRINT "File does not exist! Try again." : RESUME 20
   9030 RESUME NEXT
   ```

8. Single-Line User Defined Functions (`DEF FN`):
   ```basic
   10 DEF FN Hypotenuse(a, b) = SQR(a * a + b * b)
   20 PRINT "Hypotenuse of 3 and 4 is: "; FN Hypotenuse(3, 4)
   ```


36.5 Print Formatting with PRINT USING & String Functions
---------------------------------------------------------
One of Mallard BASIC's greatest strengths is `PRINT USING`, which gives you complete control
over numeric alignment, currency symbols, and decimal precision.

Syntax:
  `PRINT USING format_string$; value1, value2...`
  `LPRINT USING format_string$; value1, value2...` (Sends output to printer/PDF)

1. Numeric Format Specifiers:
   - `#`  Digit placeholder. Specifies field width and reserves space for each digit.
   - `.`  Decimal point position. Numbers are automatically rounded to fit.
   - `,`  Thousands separator. Places commas every three digits left of the decimal point.
   - `+`  Signs display. Prints `+` for positive numbers, `-` for negative numbers.
   - `-`  Trailing minus. Negative numbers print with a trailing `-` (standard accounting).
   - `**` Asterisk check protection. Fills leading blank spaces with asterisks (`*`).
   - `$$` Floating dollar sign. Places a dollar sign immediately before the first digit.
   - `**$` Combination check protection: fills leading spaces with `*` and prepends `$`.
   - `^^^^` Exponential / Scientific notation. Appends `E+xx`.

   Examples:
   ```basic
   PRINT USING "###.##"; 12.3456       ' Outputs:  12.35
   PRINT USING "###,###.##"; 123456.7  ' Outputs: 123,456.70
   PRINT USING "$$###.##"; 49.95       ' Outputs:   $49.95
   PRINT USING "**$###.##"; 25.50      ' Outputs: ***$25.50
   PRINT USING "+###.##"; 88.2         ' Outputs:   +88.20
   ```

2. String Format Specifiers:
   - `!`  Prints only the first character of the string.
   - `\   \` Fixed-width string field. Reserves length equal to 2 plus the number of
          spaces between the backslashes. If the string is longer, it is truncated;
          if shorter, it is padded with spaces.
   - `&`  Variable-length string field. Prints the entire string without modification.

   Examples:
   ```basic
   PRINT USING "!"; "Sinclair"              ' Outputs: S
   PRINT USING "\     "; "Spectrum +3"     ' Outputs: Spectru (7 chars)
   PRINT USING "Name: & Status: &"; "Alice"; "Active"
   ```

3. Built-In String Functions Reference:
   - `LEFT$(s$, n)`       Returns leftmost `n` characters.
   - `RIGHT$(s$, n)`      Returns rightmost `n` characters.
   - `MID$(s$, start, n)` Returns `n` characters starting from index `start` (1-based).
   - `INSTR([start, ] s1$, s2$)` Finds position of substring `s2$` inside `s1$`.
   - `STRING$(n, char)`   Returns string of `n` repeating characters.
   - `SPACE$(n)`          Returns string of `n` spaces.
   - `LEN(s$)`            Length of string.
   - `CHR$(n)`            Returns ASCII character corresponding to code `n`.
   - `ASC(s$)`            Returns ASCII code of first character of `s$`.
   - `STR$(n)`            Converts number to string with leading space for positive values.
   - `VAL(s$)`            Converts string to numeric value.
   - `HEX$(n)`            Converts integer to hexadecimal string.
   - `OCT$(n)`            Converts integer to octal string.


36.6 File I/O Subsystem (Sequential and Random Access Files with FIELD/LSET/RSET)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Mallard BASIC contains an industrial-grade file management subsystem capable of both
streaming sequential files and high-performance random-access record databases.

1. Opening and Closing Files:
   - Sequential Output (Overwrite):
       `OPEN "O", #1, "CUSTOMER.TXT"`
   - Sequential Input (Read):
       `OPEN "I", #1, "CUSTOMER.TXT"`
   - Sequential Append (Add to end of existing file):
       `OPEN "A", #1, "LOG.TXT"`
   - Random Access (Read & Write with fixed-length record buffers):
       `OPEN "R", #1, "ACCOUNTS.DAT", 64`   (Opens with 64-byte record buffer)
   - Closing Files:
       `CLOSE #1`             (Closes stream 1 and flushes buffers to disk)
       `CLOSE`                (Closes all open files)

2. Sequential File Operations:
   - `PRINT #stream, expression...`
       Writes formatted ASCII text lines to file.
   - `WRITE #stream, expr1, expr2...`
       Writes data items delimited by commas, enclosing strings in quotation marks.
   - `INPUT #stream, var1, var2...`
       Reads comma-delimited data values from file into variables.
   - `LINE INPUT #stream, string_var$`
       Reads an entire line of text (including commas and quotation marks) into a string.
   - File Status Functions:
       `EOF(stream)`   Returns -1 (True) if end of file reached; 0 (False) otherwise.
       `LOF(stream)`   Returns length of file in bytes.
       `LOC(stream)`   Returns current 128-byte record position within the file.

3. Random Access File Architecture (`FIELD`, `LSET`, `RSET`, `PUT`, `GET`):
   Random access files allow you to read or write any record instantly without scanning
   through preceding data.
   - How it works:
     1. Open file with `OPEN "R", #stream, filename, record_length`.
     2. Define the record structure using `FIELD`:
          `FIELD #1, 6 AS acc_id$, 25 AS name$, 4 AS bal$, 4 AS limit$`
     3. Prepare data in the field buffer using `LSET` (left-justify) or `RSET` (right-justify).
     4. Write record to disk with `PUT #1, record_number`.
     5. Retrieve record from disk with `GET #1, record_number`.

4. Numeric Conversion Functions for Random Files:
   To store numbers compactly inside fixed-width string buffers, Mallard BASIC provides
   binary conversion functions:
     - `MKI$(integer%)`  /  `CVI(2-byte string)`
         Converts a 16-bit integer to/from a 2-byte binary string.
     - `MKS$(single!)`   /  `CVS(4-byte string)`
         Converts a 32-bit single-precision float to/from a 4-byte binary string.
     - `MKD$(double#)`   /  `CVD(8-byte string)`
         Converts a 64-bit double-precision float to/from an 8-byte binary string.

5. Complete Hands-On Project: Customer Account Database
   Here is a complete, runnable Mallard BASIC random-access database program. You can type
   this directly into Mallard BASIC in BasinPy:
   ```basic
   10 CLS
   20 PRINT "==========================================="
   30 PRINT "   MALLARD-80 CUSTOMER ACCOUNT SYSTEM      "
   40 PRINT "==========================================="
   50 OPEN "R", #1, "ACCOUNTS.DAT", 64
   60 FIELD #1, 6 AS f_id$, 30 AS f_name$, 4 AS f_bal$, 4 AS f_limit$
   70 PRINT "1. Add New Account"
   80 PRINT "2. Read Existing Account"
   90 PRINT "3. Exit to CP/M"
   100 INPUT "Select Option (1-3): "; opt%
   110 ON opt% GOSUB 1000, 2000, 3000
   120 GOTO 70
   1000 ' Add Account Routine
   1010 INPUT "Record Number (1-100): "; rec%
   1020 INPUT "Account ID (e.g. ACC01): "; id$
   1030 INPUT "Customer Name: "; cname$
   1040 INPUT "Current Balance: "; bal!
   1050 INPUT "Credit Limit: "; lim!
   1060 LSET f_id$ = id$
   1070 LSET f_name$ = cname$
   1080 LSET f_bal$ = MKS$(bal!)
   1090 LSET f_limit$ = MKS$(lim!)
   1100 PUT #1, rec%
   1110 PRINT "Record #"; rec%; " successfully written to disk!"
   1120 RETURN
   2000 ' Read Account Routine
   2010 INPUT "Enter Record Number to Fetch: "; rec%
   2020 GET #1, rec%
   2030 PRINT "-------------------------------------------"
   2040 PRINT "Account ID   : "; f_id$
   2050 PRINT "Customer Name: "; f_name$
   2060 PRINT USING "Balance      : $$###,###.##"; CVS(f_bal$)
   2070 PRINT USING "Credit Limit : $$###,###.##"; CVS(f_limit$)
   2080 PRINT "-------------------------------------------"
   2090 RETURN
   3000 ' Exit Routine
   3010 CLOSE #1
   3020 PRINT "Database closed. Returning to CP/M..."
   3030 SYSTEM
   ```


36.7 Returning to CP/M (SYSTEM Command)
---------------------------------------
When your work in Mallard BASIC is complete:
  - Type `SYSTEM` at the `Ok` direct command prompt (or execute `SYSTEM` in your program):
      `Ok`
      `SYSTEM`
  - Mallard BASIC automatically flushes all open file buffers, commits pending changes to disk,
    releases memory, and returns cleanly to the CP/M command prompt:
      `A>`
  - From the CP/M `A>` prompt, typing `SPEC` returns you immediately to Sinclair ZX Spectrum
    BASIC with `0 OK, 0:1`.


================================================================================
 38. SECTION 37: ZX SPECTRUM NEXT (NEXTBASIC) & NEXTZXOS / ESXDOS / EXDOS SUBSYSTEM
================================================================================

BasinPy features an authentic emulation of the Sinclair ZX Spectrum Next, including
its FPGA Z80 core speeds (3.5 / 7 / 14 / 28 MHz), expanded 2MB MMU RAM banking (112 banks),
Next hardware registers ($00..$FF), Layer 2 256-color graphics modes, hardware sprites,
tilemaps, enhanced 9-bit RGB palettes (512 colors), NextBASIC language statements and
functions, as well as the NextZXOS / ESXDOS / EXDOS hierarchical file subsystem with
dot-commands (.ls, .cd, etc.) and colon-prefixed EXDOS commands (:DIR, :CD, etc.).


37.1 Machine Architecture & FPGA Z80 Core
-----------------------------------------
- Mode Invocation:
    `SPECTRUM NEXT`, `NEXT`, `ZX NEXT`, or `SPECTRUM 28`
  Switches BasinPy into ZX Spectrum Next mode:
    - CPU Speed defaults to 28 MHz (Turbo Mode 3).
    - Status bar displays `[NEXT]` badge, `ZX NEXT BASIC [K]`, and `28MHz`.
    - Memory expands to 112 16KB RAM banks (up to 2MB).
    - Standard paging unlocked, with MMU slots 0..7 pointing to authentic Next memory banks.
- CPU Turbo Control:
    `TURBO <speed>`
  Sets CPU turbo clock speed:
    - `TURBO 0` or `TURBO 3.5` : 3.5 MHz (Standard Spectrum speed)
    - `TURBO 1` or `TURBO 7`   : 7.0 MHz (2x speed)
    - `TURBO 2` or `TURBO 14`  : 14.0 MHz (4x speed)
    - `TURBO 3` or `TURBO 28`  : 28.0 MHz (8x Next maximum speed)
  The current speed can be read programmatically via `TURBO()` or `REG(7)`.


37.2 Next Video & Graphics Modes
--------------------------------
- Layer 2 Graphic Engine:
    `LAYER 2, 1` (Enable Layer 2 display)
    `LAYER 2, 0` (Disable Layer 2 display)
    `LAYER ORDER <priority>` (Set layer composition: 0=SLU, 1=LSU, 2=SUL, 3=LUS, 4=USL, 5=ULS)
    `LAYER OVER <n>`
  Layer 2 provides a linear 256-color frame buffer with independent palette mapping and scrolling.
  Status can be queried via `LAYER()` (returns 1.0 if Layer 2 is enabled, 0.0 otherwise).

- Enhanced 512-Color 9-Bit Palettes:
    `PALETTE <layer>, <index>, <rgb9>`
  Where `<layer>` is `ULA`, `LAYER2`, `SPRITE`, or `TILE`.
  `<index>` is palette entry 0..255.
  `<rgb9>` is a 9-bit color word (RRRGGGBBB) ranging 0..511.
    `PALETTE RESET`
  Restores all 4 hardware palettes to authentic system defaults.

- Next Hardware Sprites:
    `SPRITE <id>, <x>, <y>, <pattern> [, <palette>] [, <flags>]`
    `SPRITE MOVE <id>, <dx>, <dy>`
    `SPRITE FLIP <id>, <flip_x>, <flip_y>`
    `SPRITE ROTATE <id>, <rot>`
    `SPRITE LOAD "filename"`
    `SPRITE STOP`, `SPRITE CONTINUE`, `SPRITE PRINT`
  Hardware sprites operate on up to 128 independent hardware slots with automatic clipping,
  rotation (0, 90, 180, 270 deg), horizontal/vertical flipping, and priority sorting.

- Next Tilemap Subsystem:
    `TILEMAP ON` / `TILEMAP OFF`
    `TILEMAP DIM <width>, <height>`
    `TILEMAP <x>, <y>, <tile_id>`
  Enables hardware tilemap background rendering supporting 40x32 or 80x32 tile grids with
  hardware X/Y fine pixel scrolling.


37.3 Next Hardware Register Catalog (NEXTREG & REG() Complete Reference)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
Next hardware registers control core hardware features and peripherals:
    `NEXTREG <register>, <value>` (or `REG <register>, <value>`)
    `REG(<register>)` or `NEXTREG(<register>)` (Read register value)

Key Registers:
    $00 (0)  : Machine ID (Returns $0A for ZX Spectrum Next)
    $01 (1)  : Next Core Version (Returns $31 for Core 3.1)
    $02 (2)  : Next Reset / Core Control
    $05 (5)  : Peripheral 1 (50Hz / 60Hz timing, joystick configuration)
    $06 (6)  : Peripheral 2 (Audio output routing, internal speaker)
    $07 (7)  : CPU Speed / Turbo (0=3.5MHz, 1=7MHz, 2=14MHz, 3=28MHz)
    $08 (8)  : Peripheral 3 (Contention, RAM disable, DAC mode)
    $12 (18) : Layer 2 RAM Bank (Default Bank 9)
    $14 (20) : Global Transparency Color (Default $E3)
    $15 (21) : Sprite & Layer Priority Order (0..5)
    $16 (22) : Layer 2 X Scroll (0..255)
    $17 (23) : Layer 2 Y Scroll (0..191)
    $18 (24) : Clip Window Layer 2
    $19 (25) : Clip Window ULA
    $1A (26) : Clip Window Sprites
    $1B (27) : Clip Window Tilemap
    $26 (38) : ULA X Scroll
    $27 (39) : ULA Y Scroll
    $2F (47) : Tilemap X Scroll LSB
    $30 (48) : Tilemap X Scroll MSB
    $31 (49) : Tilemap Y Scroll
    $40 (64) : Palette Index
    $41 (65) : Palette Value (8-bit / 9-bit write)
    $43 (67) : Palette Control
    $50..$57 : MMU Slots 0..7 (Map 8KB RAM/ROM pages to $0000..$FFFF)


37.4 NextBASIC Extended Commands & Statements Reference
-------------------------------------------------------
- `NEXTREG <reg>, <val>` : Set Next hardware register.
- `REG <reg>, <val>`     : Alias for NEXTREG.
- `TURBO <spd>`          : Set CPU speed (0=3.5MHz, 1=7MHz, 2=14MHz, 3=28MHz).
- `LAYER 2, <state>`     : Turn Layer 2 screen ON (1) or OFF (0).
- `LAYER ORDER <n>`      : Set visual rendering order of layers.
- `PALETTE <lyr>, <i>, <c>` : Modify palette color for ULA, LAYER2, SPRITE, or TILE.
- `PALETTE RESET`        : Reset palettes to defaults.
- `BANK <n>`             : Select RAM bank 0..111.
- `BANK <n> COPY <src>`  : Copy 16KB between RAM banks.
- `BANK <b1> TO <b2>`    : Transfer bank block.
- `CLEAR BANK <n>`       : Erase/zero RAM bank.
- `PWD`                  : Print current working directory.
- `CD <path>`            : Change working directory.
- `MKDIR "dir"`          : Create subdirectory.
- `RMDIR "dir"`          : Remove subdirectory.
- `ERASE "file"`         : Delete file from current path.
- `RENAME "old" TO "new"`: Rename file.
- `COPY "src" TO "dst"`  : Copy file.
- `MOVE "src" TO "dst"`  : Move file.
- `BACKUP "src" TO "dst"`: Backup file.
- `DRIVER <id>, <cmd>`   : Call NextZXOS hardware driver.


37.5 NextBASIC Built-in Functions Reference
-------------------------------------------
- `REG(<reg>)`           : Returns current 8-bit value of Next register <reg>.
- `NEXTREG(<reg>)`       : Alias for REG(<reg>).
- `TURBO()`              : Returns current CPU turbo speed index (0..3).
- `LAYER()`              : Returns 1.0 if Layer 2 is active, 0.0 otherwise.
- `PEEK$(<addr>, <len>)` : Reads <len> bytes from memory starting at <addr> as a string.
- `DEC$(<num> [, <w>])`  : Converts numeric value to decimal string, optionally zero-padded to <w> digits.
- `EXP$(<expr_str>)`     : Evaluates an expression contained within a string and returns the result string.
- `BIN$(<num> [, <bits>]): Formats integer as a binary string representation.
- `HEX$(<num> [, <len>]) : Formats integer as a hexadecimal string representation.
- `MOD(a, b)` / `a MOD b`: Returns remainder of a divided by b.
- `a << b` and `a >> b`  : Bitwise shift left and shift right operators.
- `DRIVER(<id>, <cmd>)`  : Query NextZXOS driver status.
- `DSK$(<drive>)`        : Returns drive status and free space volume string.


37.6 NextZXOS, ESXDOS & EXDOS Disk & File Management Subsystem
--------------------------------------------------------------
BasinPy provides a safe, virtualized filesystem environment rooted in the workspace:
- Standard Statements:
    `PWD`
    `CD "/demos"` or `CD "subdir"`
    `MKDIR "projects"`
    `RMDIR "temp"`
    `COPY "game.bas" TO "game_bak.bas"`
    `RENAME "test.txt" TO "final.txt"`
    `ERASE "scratch.dat"`
- Dot-Commands:
  Quick command line utilities prefixed with `.`:
    `.ls` or `.dir`   : Directory listing of files and subdirectories.
    `.cd <path>`      : Change directory.
    `.pwd`            : Display current working path.
    `.mkdir <dir>`    : Create new directory.
    `.rmdir <dir>`    : Remove empty directory.
    `.cp <src> <dst>` : Copy file.
    `.mv <src> <dst>` : Move file.
    `.rm <file>`      : Delete file.
    `.cat <file>`     : Print contents of text file to screen.
    `.vstat`          : Display Next video and turbo hardware status.
    `.help`           : Display NextZXOS command help summary.
- Colon-Commands:
  Authentic EXDOS colon commands:
    `:DIR` or `:CAT`  : Directory catalog.
    `:CD <path>`      : Change directory.
    `:ERASE <file>`   : Delete file.
    `:TYPE <file>`    : View text file.
    `:REN <old> <new>`: Rename file.
    `:COPY <src> <dst>`: Copy file.


37.7 IDE Compliance Tiers, Status Bar Badges & Syntax Highlighting
------------------------------------------------------------------
- Compliance Tier:
    `[NEXT]` is highlighted in Emerald / Jade Green `RGB(0, 160, 120)` in the visual compliance bar.
    The command `COMPLIANCE` analyzes programs and identifies Next-specific keywords:
    `Next / EXDOS: <count>`
    Target environment: `"ZX Spectrum Next (NextBASIC / EXDOS)"`
- Status Bar Badges:
    - Operational Status: `[NEXT]` in jade green when idle.
    - Operating Mode: `ZX NEXT BASIC [K]` in cyan.
    - Hardware Indicator: `Col <col> | 28MHz | 1.8M Free`.


37.8 Complete Hands-On NextBASIC Tutorials & Programs
-----------------------------------------------------

Tutorial 1: Turbo Speed & Next Registers
```basic
10 REM Turbo & Next Hardware Registers
20 SPECTRUM NEXT
30 CLS
40 PRINT "ZX Spectrum Next Machine ID : "; HEX$(REG(0))
50 PRINT "Core Version                : "; HEX$(REG(1))
60 PRINT "Setting Turbo Mode to 28MHz..."
70 TURBO 28
80 PRINT "Current Turbo Speed Index   : "; TURBO()
90 PRINT "Register 7 Value            : "; REG(7)
100 FOR s=0 TO 3
110   TURBO s
120   PRINT "Turbo Speed "; s; " ("; [3.5,7,14,28](s+1); " MHz)"
130 NEXT s
140 TURBO 28
150 PRINT "Turbo testing complete!"
```

Tutorial 2: Next Palettes & Layer 2 Graphics
```basic
10 REM Next Palette Manipulation & Layer 2
20 SPECTRUM NEXT
30 CLS
40 PRINT "Enabling Layer 2..."
50 LAYER 2, 1
60 PRINT "Layer 2 active: "; LAYER()
70 REM Set custom 9-bit RGB palette entry 1 to Bright Red (%111000000 = 448)
80 PALETTE LAYER2, 1, 448
90 REM Set custom palette entry 2 to Bright Cyan (%000111111 = 63)
100 PALETTE LAYER2, 2, 63
110 PRINT "Custom palettes configured."
120 PAUSE 100
130 PALETTE RESET
140 LAYER 2, 0
150 PRINT "Reset to defaults."
```

Tutorial 3: Next Hardware Sprites & EXDOS File Access
```basic
10 REM Next Sprites & EXDOS Filesystem
20 SPECTRUM NEXT
30 CLS
40 PRINT "Creating projects directory..."
50 MKDIR "NEXTDEMO"
60 CD "NEXTDEMO"
70 PWD
80 REM Place sprite 0 at (100, 80) with pattern 1
90 SPRITE 0, 100, 80, 1
100 SPRITE MOVE 0, 10, -5
110 SPRITE FLIP 0, 1, 0
120 SPRITE ROTATE 0, 90
130 PRINT "Sprite 0 active and positioned."
140 CD "/"
150 PRINT "Done!"
```


================================================================================
 39. SECTION 38: USING BORIEL'S ZX BASIC (ZXBC) ON THE ZX SPECTRUM & BASINPY
